# Aspen: execution plan

Aspen is a self-hostable Git server, written in Rust, built for agent-scale
traffic. It follows the design OpenAI published for Pando ("Scaling Git at
OpenAI", https://openai.com/index/scaling-git-at-openai/, 2026-11-02):

- packs are stored in object storage without being unpacked; a thin pushed
  pack is first completed into a self-contained pack (spike S3);
- branch history lives in a database, and each branch updates independently;
- a push sees only the trunk branch;
- fetches are served by selecting stored packs, never by building one per client;
- small packs are merged in the background in levels, like an LSM tree.

It also takes inspiration from Cursor's "Git at any scale"
(https://cursor.com/blog/git-at-any-scale, August 2026): push throughput is
measured against Cursor's published numbers, counting a push only once it is
durable, and group commit batches the write that publishes pushes the way
Cursor's design does (decisions log, "Group commit"). README.md, "Where the
design comes from", says which idea came from which source.

Aspen is open source under the Apache License 2.0 and published at
https://github.com/reason-machines/aspen by a regular sync from the
maintainers' development repository. Write everything (code, docs,
commit history) as if strangers will read it. This file is the source of
truth for scope and sequencing. Update it when a spike or a review changes a
decision, and record why.

Owner: **Aspen**, a coding agent that coordinates the work: it keeps this
plan, reviews and merges every pull request, and runs each node in its own
agent session (the "Agent session" column below). Requested by the maintainers,
2026-11-02.

## 1. Product

"A Git server that stays fast when a thousand agents push at once, and that
you can run yourself on any cloud in five minutes."

Target users: teams whose agents, CI and humans hammer one repository. They
run Aspen in one of two ways:

- **Primary mode:** Aspen is the Git host.
- **Mirror mode:** Aspen sits in front of GitHub or GitLab. Fetches come from
  Aspen's own packs, and pushes are forwarded upstream either synchronously
  (proxy) or in the background (Aspen is primary, upstream is a downstream
  mirror).

Mirror mode is the main adoption path, because nobody has to migrate.

### Goals

- Works with stock `git`, over HTTP and SSH, with no client changes required.
- Pushes to different branches scale horizontally. The only contended write is
  a compare-and-swap on one branch's tip.
- Reads scale to the network limit. Clients that set
  `git config fetch.uriprotocols https` download packs straight from object
  storage or a CDN.
- Runs anywhere: one binary, Postgres, and any S3-compatible bucket (S3, GCS,
  Azure Blob, R2, MinIO, or the local filesystem).
- Reliability is proven by differential testing against stock Git, chaos
  testing and fuzzing, not asserted.

### Not in v1 (document each one, and give a clear error where needed)

- Partial-clone filters: don't advertise `filter`, so clients fall back to a
  full fetch (verified in S5). Lazy fetches from existing partial clones
  (`want <blob-oid>`) get a clear `ERR`; mirror mode may forward them upstream
  later.
- Exact shallow packs for every deepen form: a plain `--depth=N` fetch of a
  ref version's tip (N up to 64) is served an exact pack, built once per
  version and depth (node C8). `--deepen` (relative), `--shallow-since`,
  `--shallow-exclude`, `--unshallow`, deeper depths and non-tip wants are
  still served the full history of the wants plus an exact `shallow-info`
  (S5). Operators can set `shallow = refuse` to answer with a clear `ERR`
  instead, or `ASPEN_SHALLOW_PACKS=false` to serve every shallow fetch the
  full history.
- SHA-256 repositories.
- A web UI for browsing code.
- Code review or issues.

---

## 2. Architecture

```
git client ─HTTP/SSH─► aspen-server ─CAS per ref─► Postgres
                            │                      (refs, ref_versions, commit_index, covers)
                            │ stream+validate
                            ▼
                     object store (S3/GCS/Azure/R2/MinIO/fs) ◄── aspen-compactor (L0→L4 covers)
                            ▲
git client ◄─packfile-uri── aspen-express (regional pack cache) / CDN
```

### Data model (schema v1, N1)

The tables below are schema v1, in
[crates/aspen-meta/migrations](../crates/aspen-meta/migrations) (Postgres and
SQLite, kept identical) and described column by column in
[crates/aspen-meta/SCHEMA.md](../crates/aspen-meta/SCHEMA.md). The Rust
contracts are in `crates/aspen-core`. Object IDs are 20-byte binary,
timestamps are microseconds since the epoch, and every cross-table reference
inside a repository is a composite foreign key through `repo_id`.

| Table | Holds |
|---|---|
| `repos` | Repository settings, including trunk ref, force policy (`allow_force`: `none`, `branches` (default; never trunk) or `all`; a forced trunk update keeps trunk exact under B1's forced-trunk rule, decisions log) and writable ref namespaces (default `refs/heads/*`, `refs/tags/*`) |
| `refs` | `(repo, name) → current ref_version_id, seq`; this is the only compare-and-swap target, and its foreign key on `(version, repo, name, seq)` plus `SET seq = seq + 1` lets a swap land only on the expected version's child. A deleted ref keeps its row, pointing at the deletion version |
| `ref_versions` | `id, repo, ref, seq, parent_version, tip_oid, closure_exact, pack_id, trunk_dep, scoped_dep, created_at`. `closure_exact` says whether the version's closure is exactly what its tip reaches, which decides whether a fetch have may stand for it (`fetch::HaveVersion`); it is computed per write against the pack that write's version names, which B1's placement rule chooses (decisions log, "where a write's objects are stored"). `seq` numbers a ref's versions from 1, and `parent_version` is the version with `seq - 1`. `tip_oid` null is a deletion; `pack_id` null means the push stored no pack. The deps are typed: `trunk_dep` is the exact trunk version the push's lookup and connectivity check searched (push path, step 4), `scoped_dep` the scoped ref's old version for a scoped-URL push (S2), and for a client's push of one branch through the plain repository URL that is not forced or leased, that branch's version the push searched, so it stores only what the branch's history lacks (issue #203). Parent and deps have smaller IDs, so the graph is acyclic. Invariant: every committed ref version is **connected**: every object reachable from `tip_oid` exists in its pack or its deps' closure (enforced by the push connectivity check) |
| `packs` | `id, repo, checksum, generation, pack_key, size, idx_key, idx_size, entries_key, entries_size, object_count, ref_delta_count, level, origin, received_bytes`. Keys are `repos/<repo>/packs/<checksum>.{pack,idx,entries}` for generation 0 and `<checksum>.g<n>.{pack,idx,entries}` for generation n, the next registration of a checksum whose earlier row garbage collection swept; `checksum` is unique per repository among live rows, and readers always take keys from the row, never from the checksum alone. `received_bytes` (push packs only) against `size` shows completion growth (S4); `ref_delta_count` gates `no-ref-delta` (S3). Writers **register, then upload, then reference**: `register_pack` returns the row (and so its generation and keys), the pack, `.idx` and entry table are uploaded to those keys, and only then may a version or cover name the row; so every row a version or cover names can be served, while an unnamed row may lack its blobs (decisions log, C1 pack generations). Invariant: every stored pack is **self-contained**: no delta base lies outside the pack (thin pushes are completed at ingest, S3; compaction writes self-contained packs). So any selected pack can be served alone: as a packfile URI, which the client indexes on its own, or as one segment of a stitched pack. Second invariant: every stored pack is **base-first**: each delta's base entry comes before the delta (S1; ingest places each completion base before the first delta that uses it and moves any received delta that precedes its base to just after it, and compaction must write base-first too). With both, the stitcher deduplicates in one streaming pass. A pack is *not* closed under reachability: objects its trees and commits reference may live in its `deps` closure |
| `commit_index` | `(repo, commit_oid) → ref_version_id, parent oids (concatenated 20-byte IDs), committer time`, written synchronously for the tip and asynchronously for the rest of a large push. `ref_version_id` is a version whose tip reaches the commit: it resolves a want, never a have. Parents and time serve `shallow-info` and fast-forward checks without reading packs (S5). A walk that reaches a commit with no row yet reads that commit from the packs of the ref version it came from (`pack_id`, then `deps`), which hold every ancestor of its tip; a missing row is never read as "no parents" or "not an ancestor". If the commit is in neither place, the request fails with a retryable error |
| `covers` | `repo, ref, level (1–4), first_seq, last_seq, pack_id or null` (null until compacted, and then a compaction pack at the cover's level): a range of one ref's version chain, aligned by `CoverLayout` (level k spans `16^k` versions). Migration 0005 adds `cover_chains`: a cover whose range is a **dependency chain** (every version after the first depends on its parent) records the ID of its first version, which a foreign key ties to the cover's ref and `first_seq`. Covers follow a ref's version history, closures follow dependencies, so a fetch treats a cover's range as held for a have only when it carries a chain (decisions log, "C1: cover chains") |
| `shallow_packs` | Migration 0006 (node C8): `repo, version, depth → pack_id, recorded_at_us`, the **exact shallow pack** of a ref version at a depth: exactly the commits fewer than `depth` parents below the version's tip (the *region*), the tags the tip peels through, and every tree and blob the region's trees reach. The pack is an ordinary `packs` row (registered as a compaction pack at level 1 that no cover names) and obeys every stored-pack invariant. The first recording wins; the sweep counts a recorded pack as named; garbage collection evicts recordings of versions that are no longer current after a TTL (decisions log, C8 rows). Migration 0007 adds `used_at_us`, a recording's last use: garbage collection also evicts recordings unused for a day, current or not, and each repository's recorded packs stay under a byte cap, least recently used evicted first (issue #108) |
| `jobs` | Background work queue (`index_commits`, `compact`, `mirror_push` on a ref version; `rewrite_pack` on a pack that broke base-first), written in the push's own transaction and enqueued idempotently (one job that is not done per kind and target). States `queued`, `running` (leased, fenced on worker and attempt), `done`, `dead` (after 10 attempts, failed or expired). A dead job is requeued by resolving it and enqueueing a fresh job in one transaction, never by resetting `attempts` |

### Push path

Confirmed against git 2.30, 2.39, 2.56 and OpenAI's fork by spike S2. The
full rule table and the decisive wire excerpts are in
[bench/spikes/s2/RESULT.md](../bench/spikes/s2/RESULT.md).

1. The client connects and Aspen runs v0/v1 receive-pack. It advertises trunk
   as the only named ref, plus earlier trunk tips as `.have` lines, or
   `capabilities^{}` for an empty repository: every one of the newest
   `8 × have_tips` trunk versions (64 by default), then tips about a
   quarter apart down to the oldest trunk version a push searches
   (`meta::have_tip_seqs`; at most 108 lines for a million versions).
   Without the `.have` lines, a client that has not fetched the latest
   trunk pushes its whole history; with them, a client any number of
   landings behind resends at most about a quarter of them (decisions log,
   "item 10"). Capabilities: `report-status delete-refs side-band-64k
   quiet atomic ofs-delta push-options object-format=sha1 agent`.
   - A **branch-scoped URL**, `/<repo>.git/-/refs/heads/<branch>`, also
     advertises that one ref. It is the opt-in route for stock
     `--force-with-lease` and `git push --delete <branch>`.
   - **`push.negotiate`.** Through the plain URL, stock Git resends every
     commit of the pushed branch that is not on trunk. With
     `git config push.negotiate true` it first runs a negotiation-only v2
     fetch (`wait-for-done`, haves, no wants); Aspen advertises
     `wait-for-done` and acknowledges every have with a `commit_index` row,
     and Git leaves those commits out. A fast-forward of the pushed branch
     then needs nothing beyond its usual dependencies (issue #203's branch
     dependency). A push whose left-out commits are outside its
     dependencies (another branch's commits) is completed from their
     versions (decisions log, "push.negotiate").
2. Ingest produces a **self-contained** pack, which packfile URIs require
   (spike S3), and never unpacks it. It is synchronous and takes two passes:
   - **Pass 1 (receive):** the incoming pack streams to a spool file on
     local disk (a real disk, never tmpfs) while `gix-pack` parses it. It
     inflates every entry, checks each entry's CRC32, and checks the
     client's trailer with SHA-1 collision detection. Memory stays under
     10 MiB at any pack size; the parse runs at about 110 MB/s per core
     (S4). The base IDs of all REF_DELTA entries are collected as
     **candidate bases**. A candidate may be a thin base (in `deps`) or an
     object in the same pack: libgit2 sends every delta as REF_DELTA, mostly
     against bases in the pack (S1's `push_order.py`), and pass 1 cannot
     tell the two apart, because a deltified entry's ID is known only once
     it is resolved. A push whose header object count exceeds the limit is
     refused before its body is read.
   - **Base prefetch:** each candidate, with its delta chain, is looked up
     in parallel before completion starts. The base-object lookup probes
     the `.idx` of the covering packs of every ref version advertised on
     the connection: trunk, plus the branch for a branch-scoped URL, which
     are exactly the push's `deps` (see
     [S3's README](../bench/spikes/s3/README.md#base-object-lookup)). A
     candidate that `deps` doesn't have is **not an error**: it is expected
     to be in the pack. Prefetching matters because gix asks for bases one
     at a time from inside its parse. A lookup that went to the blob store
     there would serialize thousands of round trips per push: S4 counted
     5,431 entry reads for a 100-commit Linux push. The prefetch is
     designed, not measured: S4's harness used a synchronous lookup over a
     local pack, and B1 measures the prefetch against a real bucket.
     Every stored pack's delta chains are known before they are read
     (chain maps from its entry table: in memory beside a whole `.idx`,
     on the spool disk beside a sampled one), so all the entries of every
     candidate's chain are read in one round of parallel range reads,
     whatever the chain's depth (decisions log, item 11).
   - **Completion and index:** `gix-pack` re-reads the spool with a lookup
     that answers only from the prefetched bases. For each REF_DELTA whose
     base the lookup returns, gix inserts the base as a full object just
     before that delta and turns the delta into an OFS_DELTA. So every
     completion base comes before the entries that use it (S1's base-first
     rule); placing all of them in front of the received entries, as S1
     first proposed, is not needed. A REF_DELTA the lookup has no answer
     for stays a REF_DELTA and gix resolves it inside the pack, in either
     direction: gix accepts a base that comes later in the pack. gix then
     rewrites the header count, re-hashes the pack (about 1 GB/s), and
     resolves every delta in parallel. That computes each object ID, again
     with SHA-1 collision detection, and writes the `.idx`. A REF_DELTA
     whose base is in neither `deps` nor the pack fails the push with
     `unpack error`. Every push takes this path: for a pack that needs no
     bases it costs 4% more CPU and returns the same bytes (S4).
   - **No object twice.** gix asks the lookup about every REF_DELTA base it
     did not insert itself, without checking whether the pack already holds
     that object. So when a pack carries object X plus a REF_DELTA against
     X, and `deps` also has X, completion stores X twice. S4 reproduced this
     ([`dup.sh`](../bench/spikes/s4/dup.sh)): a full-history resend written
     all-REF_DELTA, as libgit2 writes deltas, came out with 92 entries for
     90 objects, and `git index-pack --strict` rejected it. The rule:
     after indexing, one linear scan of the sorted `.idx` looks for an ID
     that appears twice. If any does, completion runs again over the spool
     with a lookup that answers "not found" for those IDs, so their deltas
     resolve against the copy the client sent, and the scan runs again. On
     the same pack that gives 90 unique objects, accepted by
     `--strict`. Filtering the lookup during pass 1 is not enough on its
     own: pass 1 can hash full objects but not deltified ones, so a
     deltified X would still be inserted twice. The scan is needed anyway
     as the guarantee, and the rerun is a rare path (a client resending
     objects the server has, with REF_DELTA against them).
   - **A pack that itself holds an object twice is rejected.** If the scan
     after the rerun still finds a repeated ID, the client sent both
     copies, and no lookup answer can remove either. The push fails with
     `unpack error` naming the duplicated object, and no `packs` row is
     written. This is a deliberate difference from stock `git
     receive-pack`, which accepts such a pack unless `--strict` (or
     `receive.fsckObjects`) is on. No tested client sends one (S1, S4),
     and storing it would break the no-duplicates invariant the stitcher
     relies on.
   - **Forward bases in the received entries are repaired, never
     rejected** (S1). A client's own pack may put a REF_DELTA before the
     entry it is based on; stock `git receive-pack` accepts that, so
     rejecting it would break pushes that work everywhere else. gix stores
     such a pack as it came, with the delta before its base, which would
     later trip the fetch path's forward-base `ERR` and its page. So the
     repair runs **after** completion, the duplicate check and the `.idx`,
     on gix's output:
     - *Detection.* Only an entry that is still a REF_DELTA can point
       forward (OFS_DELTA distances always point back). The skip decision
       uses the **final** completion run: if its lookup answered every
       candidate (no prefetch miss, and no ID refused by a duplicate
       rerun), no REF_DELTA remains and the check is skipped; this is every
       stock `git push`, which sends OFS_DELTA. A duplicate rerun refuses
       IDs on purpose, so the REF_DELTAs against them stay and can point
       forward; after a rerun the check always runs.
       Otherwise a header walk of the completed pack (entry boundaries from
       the sorted `.idx` offsets, no inflation) compares each remaining
       REF_DELTA's offset with its base's offset from the `.idx`.
     - *Repair.* If a base comes later, the pack is rewritten on local
       disk: entries keep their order, except that each entry whose base
       comes later (and, transitively, any entry based on it) moves to just
       after its base. Compressed data is copied unchanged; OFS_DELTA
       headers whose distance changed, and the CRC32 of each changed entry,
       are recomputed. The new pack is re-hashed (about 1 GB/s) and its
       `.idx` rebuilt from the known IDs, without resolving deltas again.
     S1 found no client that sends forward bases: git 2.30–2.56, JGit,
     libgit2 and go-git all put every base first in 32 pushes
     ([`push_order.py`](../bench/spikes/s1/push_order.py)). So the repair
     is a rare path that has to be correct, not a fast one. Neither the
     header walk nor the rewrite has been measured.
   - **Connectivity check**, as `git receive-pack` does. Only objects that
     the incoming pack references (commit parents and trees, tree entries,
     tag targets) but does not contain are checked. Each must exist in the
     push's dependency closure, found with the same base-object lookup,
     batched. Nothing reachable from those objects is walked: every
     committed ref version is connected (see `ref_versions`), so existence
     implies connectivity. A missing object fails the push.
   - **Pass 2 (upload):** a multi-ref push may store more than one pack:
     B1 places each write's objects in trunk's pack, its own pack or one
     shared pack (decisions log, "where a write's objects are stored").
     For each stored pack, one sequential read feeds a multipart upload, and
     the `.idx` and the entry table (fetch path step 4) are uploaded beside
     it. The trailer of each stored pack becomes its `packs.checksum`.
     Each pack is registered before its upload, which goes to the keys of
     the returned row (decisions log, C1 pack generations).
   - Only once the pack and `.idx` are durable may step 4 run. Every
     `packs` row is self-contained, for every reader, with no flag.

   The trunk version is searched because its `.have` tips are trunk
   ancestors; the scoped branch because the client built its pack against it
   too. The per-push probe cost bound is in
   [bench/spikes/s2/RESULT.md](../bench/spikes/s2/RESULT.md#for-n1).

   Thin packs stay accepted on the wire; `no-thin` is an optional
   per-repository advertisement (S2).

   Costs that S4 measured on Linux, a 6.55 GB pack with 12.0 M objects
   ([bench/spikes/s4/README.md](../bench/spikes/s4/README.md)):
   - CPU per push, the whole pipeline: pass 1 (64 CPU-seconds with the
     spool) plus completion and index over the spool (282), about 346
     CPU-seconds, against 680 for `git index-pack`, which does both in one
     process. The duplicate scan is one pass over the `.idx`. Each rare
     path adds its own cost: a duplicate rerun repeats the 282, and a
     forward-base repair adds a local copy and a re-hash. Prefetch I/O and
     the upload come on top and are measured by B1.
   - Memory: about 140 bytes of heap per object (1.6 GiB), plus the
     mmapped pack, which is reclaimable page cache.
   - Disk: about twice the push, plus its `.idx`.
   - Stored size: completion grows packs most for small pushes, from
     320 B to 37 kB for one commit and from 10.5 MB to 57 MB for 100
     commits. Git's `--fix-thin` grows them the same way.

   B1 measures push latency end to end, against a real bucket.

   Test obligations for step 2. A2 covers each with a unit test on
   constructed packs, and B1 covers each end to end through `git push` and
   the libgit2 client in A6's matrix. Each stored pack must index under stock
   `git index-pack` without `--fix-thin`, and no ID may repeat in its
   `.idx` (`--strict` also rejects a repeat, but it checks connectivity
   too, so it only fits a test pack that is closed):
   - an all-REF_DELTA pack whose bases are in the pack and not in `deps`
     (an ordinary libgit2 push): every candidate misses in `deps` and the
     push is accepted;
   - an all-REF_DELTA resend of objects `deps` already has, including a
     REF_DELTA against a deltified object of the pack (S4's `dup.sh` is the
     model): no duplicate is stored;
   - a REF_DELTA before its in-pack base, with and without that base also
     in `deps`: stored base-first, no duplicate;
   - a REF_DELTA whose base is in neither the pack nor `deps`:
     `unpack error`, and no `packs` row;
   - a received pack that holds the same object twice: `unpack error`
     naming that object, and no `packs` row.
3. The commit check runs. The client never saw any branch except trunk, so
   (full table in RESULT.md):
   - **Create:** accepted.
   - **Existing branch, zero old-oid** (every update of an unadvertised
     branch looks like this): Aspen runs the fast-forward check itself
     against `commit_index`, falling back to the ref version's packs for
     commits not indexed yet (see the `commit_index` row). If ancestry can't
     be decided, the update gets a retryable `ng`, never a guessed verdict.
     If `new` equals the current tip, it is a no-op.
   - **Non-fast-forward:** `git push --force` is invisible to the server.
     Accept only with push option `-o force` (or `force=<ref>`) or a matching
     `-o lease=<ref>:<oid>`, and only where `allow_force` permits.
   - **Delete:** clients refuse `--delete <short-name>` for unadvertised
     branches. `--delete refs/heads/<branch>` arrives with a zero old-oid, so
     it is unconditional: like a force, it needs `-o force` and `allow_force`.
     A delete with a real old-oid (branch-scoped URL, or a lease) is a
     compare-and-swap delete and needs no force. Trunk cannot be deleted.
   - **Tags** never fast-forward: replacing one needs `-o force`.
   - **Exact replay:** a command refused only because the ref already
     moved can still be `ok`. It is when the ref's current tip is the
     command's new value (or it is deleted, for a delete), the version just
     before it had the command's old value (or its lease's), and the
     command's own rules (lease, force signal and policy, fast-forward, tag
     and trunk protection), checked against that previous version, accept
     it: answer `ok` and write nothing. libcurl resends a receive-pack POST
     when a reused connection dies before any answer, so the first copy
     can land with its answer lost (#132).
     This matches the outcome, not the request: another push that made the
     same move, and that this command would also have been allowed to
     make, counts too. Only the current version's parent counts; if the ref
     moved again, the parent cannot be read, or the command's rules refuse
     the move, the command keeps its refusal. Creates take no part: a zero
     old value claims nothing, so a resent create is checked like any
     zero-old command (a plain no-op while the ref is still at its value).
   - **Atomic:** all commands in one transaction, or all rejected.
   - Rejection reasons go in the `ng` line and on side-band 2, even under
     `quiet` (every non-terminal client asks for it).

   Every verdict (fast-forward, lease, delete, no-op) is computed against one
   exact `ref_version` of the target ref, and that version is the one the
   step 4 compare-and-swap expects.
4. One transaction inserts the `ref_version`, sets the `refs` row with
   compare-and-swap, and enqueues jobs. That makes the push read-your-own-write
   with O(1) metadata reads and writes. If the compare-and-swap loses because
   the ref moved, Aspen recomputes the step 3 verdict against the new version
   (a bounded number of times), or returns a retryable `ng`. It never commits
   a verdict computed against a stale version. A3's and B1's tests, and the
   differential suite, cover two concurrent pushes to one branch.

   The pack's trunk dependency, recorded in `deps`, is the exact trunk
   version that step 2's lookup and connectivity check searched. It is
   never re-read at commit time: where a repository allows trunk force,
   trunk can be rewound between step 2 and this step, and a re-read would
   record a version whose packs were never searched. Smart HTTP does not
   tell the server which trunk tip it advertised, so a pack built against
   a rewound tip is caught by that connectivity check, not assumed away;
   trunk being fast-forward-only by default only makes this rare.

   A **forced trunk update** (only with `allow_force = all`) is the one
   exception, fixed at push time by B1 (decisions log): its `trunk_dep` is
   the newest trunk version whose tip is an ancestor of the new tip, its
   pack also holds the objects the new tip reaches that are only in the
   skipped versions' closures, and orphan history stores its full closure
   with no trunk dependency. The new version is then exact.

### Fetch path (protocol v2)

1. `ls-refs` honours `ref-prefix`, so the server never sends a 100 MB ref list.
   Each instance keeps a repository's refs in memory once it has listed
   them, and every later `ls-refs` asks the store only which refs changed
   since (`MetaStore::ref_changes`, one statement over
   `ref_versions.txid`), so polls by stock `git ls-remote <url> <ref>`,
   which sends no `ref-prefix`, cost one statement and no ref rows. A
   listing is never stale: each request is answered from a store read
   that began after it arrived (decisions log, "ls-refs snapshots").
2. `fetch` maps the client's wants to ref versions through `commit_index`,
   and each have to a version only when that version's closure is exact and
   its tip is the have or an ancestor of it (`fetch::HaveVersion`); other
   haves are dropped, which only costs bytes. The planner then picks the
   covering packs, using the cover levels as a skip list, so the pack count
   grows logarithmically with the versions sent. On the have side the walk
   skips through chained covers (C1): when it expands a held version at
   `seq = s` that lies inside a cover carrying a chain, every version of
   that ref from the cover's first to `s` is held (each depends on its
   parent, so its closure contains the earlier ones'), and the walk jumps
   to the cover's first version instead of stepping down one version at a
   time. A client current on trunk fetching a branch created N trunk
   pushes ago visits a handful of versions per cover level plus the
   uncompacted tail, not N. The want side is still linear: a full clone
   visits every version of its closure. B2 exports `PlanStats.visited` as a
   metric; `PlanStats.chain_skips` (how many cover chains the walk jumped
   through) is in the planner's API for its tests and for D2, which
   benchmarks the stale-branch fetch (section 4). A cover without a chain never changes what the walk
   treats as held (decisions log, cover-subtraction rule and "C1: cover
   chains").
3. Clients that set `fetch.uriprotocols` get an empty inline pack plus
   `packfile-uris` that point at presigned URLs or the CDN. Rules from spike
   S3 (`bench/spikes/s3/README.md`):
   - the advertised hash is the pack's trailing SHA-1 (`packs.checksum`);
   - only URIs whose scheme the client listed (clients do not filter);
   - always a `packfile` section, even when it is empty;
   - URLs are direct (no redirect), self-authorizing and honour `Range`;
   - direct-to-bucket URIs go only to clients whose `agent=` parses as
     `git/2.56` or later. Older opted-in clients get URIs only on an
     endpoint with the `206` rule below: `aspen-express`, or a CDN tier
     declared `ASPEN_CDN_PAST_END=206`. A CDN tier declared `416` (the
     default) is for `git/2.56` and later only. With no such endpoint they
     get the stitched pack. The agent string is self-reported, so this is
     a reliability choice, not security;
   - each planned pack goes out as an express or CDN URI first, then as the
     store's own URL, then stitched into the inline pack (decisions log,
     "C2: delivery order per planned pack");
   - an Aspen-served blob endpoint answers a range at or past the end with
     `206` and an empty body, not `416` (git ≤ 2.55 gets stuck on `416`);
   - advertise `no-ref-delta` only when every planned pack is REF_DELTA-free
     (open for B2: over smart HTTP the advertisement precedes the request,
     so B2 sets the policy; see the decisions log);
   - soft cap on URIs per response, with a stitched pack beyond it.
4. Every other client gets a **stitched pack**, built from the selected
   packs' entries in plan order:
   - drop every entry whose object id was already emitted (keep the first
     copy);
   - copy every other entry's compressed data unchanged; rewrite only the
     OFS_DELTA base distance, against wherever the base copy was emitted;
   - write the header object count (known from the plan before the first
     byte) and recompute the trailing SHA-1.

   Invariants of every stitched pack, enforced by A2 and its fuzz target:
   each object id at most once, every delta's base earlier in the pack, and
   never thin (stored packs are self-contained, and go-git never asks for
   `thin-pack`). A2 also measures the maximum delta chain depth of stitched
   packs: keep-first can hang a delta in a later segment off an earlier
   segment's copy of its base that is itself a delta, so depth can approach
   the sum of the segments' depths. A2 checks that git 2.30 `index-pack`
   accepts the worst case the compaction levels allow. Spike S1 (`bench/spikes/s1/`) showed why: plain
   concatenation is accepted by every client only when no object repeats.
   Overlapping covers and completed push bases do repeat objects, and then
   libgit2 rejects the pack, git 2.30–2.47 reject it when the repeated
   object is a REF_DELTA base, every git rejects it with `fsckObjects`, and
   go-git writes a pack stock git can't read. Naive deduplication can make
   delta cycles that exhaust the client's memory. With base-first stored
   packs (`packs` invariant), keep-first deduplication emits every base
   before its deltas in a single pass.

   The production stitcher is single-pass and never reorders or buffers
   entries. A delta whose base is neither earlier in its own segment nor
   already emitted (a forward base) means a stored pack broke the
   base-first `packs` invariant: the fetch fails with a retryable `ERR`.
   A retry can't heal it, because the stored pack is still wrong, so the
   event is also an alert (an error metric that pages), not just a log
   line. The fix is to rewrite that pack base-first with a compaction or
   repair job, not to wait for clients to retry. Base-first is enforced
   where packs are written, at ingest (push path step 2) and in
   compaction, never repaired at fetch time.

   Memory: finding the duplicate ids is a k-way merge of the segments'
   sorted `.idx` files, in memory proportional to the number of segments.
   Emitting is not: a kept OFS_DELTA whose base copy was dropped needs the
   output offset of the base's first copy, and dropped entries and resized
   OFS_DELTA headers shift every later entry. The stitcher therefore holds
   every duplicated id with its emitted offset, plus the current segment's
   old-offset to new-offset map: O(duplicates + entries in the largest
   segment). Nothing is inflated or recompressed. The
   stitcher also needs each entry's kind, header length and base, from a
   per-pack **entry table** written at ingest and by compaction, next to
   the `.idx` (`<checksum>.entries`; format in `aspen_core::pack::EntryTable`):
   one sequential read per segment, no range reads into the pack (N1).
5. **Shallow requests** (`deepen*` lines, or `shallow` lines from a shallow
   client) ignore the client's haves and plan the full closure of the wants.
   An exact `shallow-info`, computed from `commit_index` parents, goes before
   the pack. Using the haves of a shallow client is unsafe: it drops objects
   below the client's boundary (S5, `bench/spikes/s5/`). `filter` is never
   advertised, and a want that isn't a known commit or tag gets `ERR`.

   The `shallow-info` walk (depth N, `deepen-since`, `deepen-not`,
   `deepen-relative`) can reach non-tip commits of a large push whose
   `commit_index` rows are still being written by the background job. For
   those it reads the commit from the ref version's packs (`pack_id`, then
   `deps`). If a commit can't be resolved either way, the fetch fails with a
   retryable `ERR`. Aspen never emits a `shallow-info` computed from a
   partial walk: a wrong boundary corrupts the client's shallow file.

   A plain `deepen <n>` request (no `deepen-relative`, `n` up to 64, at most
   8 wants, each the tip of the version it resolves to) is not planned at
   all: each want's version is served its **exact shallow pack** for that
   depth (`shallow_packs`, node C8), built on first use and shared by every
   later request (`aspen_server::upload_pack::exact`). Several wants get the
   union of their packs, which is exact because the region of a set of
   wants is the union of their regions. `shallow-info` is computed exactly
   as above. A missing pack is built once per process at a time (single
   flight). The build's sources are the version's layered answer (below)
   when it has one, else the full-closure plan; it runs the same
   `by_depth` walk as `shallow-info` for the region, and the C1 repack
   writer restricted to the region's objects, and verifies the result
   before it is registered, uploaded and recorded (register, then upload,
   then reference). A request waits for the build up to 500 ms; then, if
   the version has a **layered answer**, it is served that while the build
   goes on (`aspen_server::upload_pack::layered`; decisions log, "Layered
   shallow answers"), one stitched pack (no packfile URIs) of a patch
   pack and a base: the base is the exact pack recorded for the newest
   earlier version of the same ref at the same depth (up to 256 versions
   back) with its own commits and tags left out, and the patch holds every
   other object the region reaches (the new commits, the trees and blobs
   changed since, and anything only older history holds, such as a
   revert's blob), read from the packs pushed since or the version's
   closure. Every object sent resolves its links, so clients with
   `fetch.fsckObjects` accept it. Only a request with one want is served a
   layered answer. Without a
   layered answer (a cold start: no version of the ref within reach has a
   recorded pack) it waits up to 20 s in all and then falls back to the
   full closure while the build goes on. Every other shallow request is
   served the full closure, as before. Measured
   (decisions log, "C8: depth-1 storm"): 0.9998× stock `git upload-pack`'s
   bytes per fetch over 1,000 concurrent depth-1 fetches.

### Compaction

- Every push produces an L0 pack.
- Covers are aligned per ref (`aspen_core::meta::CoverLayout`): level k
  spans `16^k` versions, so the cover at level k ending at `seq = s` exists
  when `s` is a multiple of `16^k`. A push enqueues a `compact` job for
  each new version whose `seq` ends a level-1 cover; the worker
  (`crates/aspen-compact`) builds every cover ending there, the covers
  below first, records each range's dependency chain when it has one, and
  repacks the cover's sources (the packs of the covers one level below, or
  the versions' own packs at level 1) into one compaction pack holding
  exactly their objects, base-first, self-contained, with delta chains of
  at most 50, and at most 10 for trees (the bases pushes need; decisions
  log, item 11). The pack is verified (`verify_pack`, plus its `.idx` and
  entry table rebuilt from its bytes and its object set against the
  sources' `.idx` files) before it is uploaded.
- Packs are never deleted while any plan might select them: compaction
  keeps the L0 packs it repacks. GC deletes only orphaned packs (no
  version, cover or job names them) after a grace period, 2 hours by
  default (issue #48; `crates/aspen-meta/SCHEMA.md`, "Garbage
  collection"; issue #203), never less than twice the longest packfile
  URL lifetime (`CompactConfig::validate`). A pass runs every 5 minutes
  in one process at a time, under a lease in the database; the sweep is
  incremental, reading registrations in the order their grace periods end
  from where it stopped, in transactions of at most 2 × 1,000 rows, on a
  pool of its own (decisions log, "GC sweep incremental and leased"). A
  pass repeats each step while it has work, up to 50 batches (the sweep:
  until it catches up) and while it has run less than 30 seconds (every
  step always runs one), so it collects everything due without holding up
  jobs.
- The same worker runs `index_commits` jobs (commit rows for large pushes).
- **Keeping up** (decisions log, 2026-11-07): a job reads only the covers
  inside the highest cover it builds (keyed, `covers_at`), never all of
  its ref's covers, so its metadata work does not grow with the ref's
  history; `ASPEN_ROLE` (`all`, `serve`, `compact`) runs the worker in its
  own processes, scaled apart from the Git servers; fetches that send a
  ref's versions as their own push packs raise that ref's queued
  `compact` jobs' priority (`jobs.priority`, migration 0008), so a backlog
  builds the covers fetches read first; `aspen_compaction_backlog`
  (queued plus running `compact` jobs) is flat when compaction keeps up.
- Exact shallow packs (C8) are built by the fetch path with the compactor's
  repack writer and checks (`Compactor::build_shallow_pack`), not by a job.
  Garbage collection evicts recordings of superseded versions after
  `shallow_ttl` (10 minutes) and touches their packs, so the sweep deletes
  a pack a whole grace period after its eviction: at most `shallow_ttl +
  gc_grace + 2 × gc_every` (2 h 20 min by default,
  `CompactConfig::reclaim_bound`) after its version was superseded, while
  each pass finishes what is due (issue #203). Recordings no fetch has used
  for `shallow_unused` (24 hours, at least 10 minutes) are evicted too,
  current versions included, and each repository's recorded packs stay
  under `ASPEN_SHALLOW_MAX_REPO_BYTES` (8 GiB), least recently used evicted
  first when a new one is recorded (issue #108).

### Crates (Cargo workspace)

```
crates/aspen-core      types + traits (MetaStore, BlobStore, Planner), errors
crates/aspen-proto     pkt-line, v0/v1 receive-pack, v2 ls-refs/fetch, v0 upload-pack (A1b), sideband, caps
crates/aspen-pack      streaming ingest/validate/idx (gix-pack), commit extraction, stitcher
crates/aspen-meta      Postgres (sqlx) + SQLite impls, migrations
crates/aspen-blob      object_store wrapper, multipart, presign
crates/aspen-planner   pure fetch planning, property-tested
crates/aspen-compact   cover scheduler + repack worker, exact shallow pack builds
crates/aspen-express   pack cache, consistent-hash routing
crates/aspen-mirror    GitHub/GitLab import + push-through
crates/aspen-auth      tokens, OIDC/JWT, SSH keys, per-repo ACL, quotas
crates/aspen-server    axum HTTP + russh SSH, wiring, health, metrics
crates/aspen-cli       aspen dev|serve|express|repo|token|import|compact|doctor
testkit/               git-client harness, differential oracle vs `git http-backend`, Aspen as a server under test (L0)
tests/{interop,chaos,e2e}/
fuzz/                  fuzz targets (aspen-fuzz) and their cargo-fuzz harness (fuzz/libfuzzer)
bench/                 aspen-bench load generator, baselines, published results
deploy/{docker,compose,helm,terraform/{aws,gcp,azure,hetzner-minio}}/
demo/place/            public pixel canvas (web, tile rules, merger bot; server/ is aspen-place)
demo/dashboard/        live metrics page
site/                  docs + benchmark write-up
```

Dependency rule: `aspen-core` depends on nothing internal. The planner is
pure: no I/O, and it depends only on core. Server and CLI are Aspen's
composition roots; `aspen-place` (the demo back end, C7) is an application
built on them and a root too. Only tooling and other roots depend on a
root; only tooling depends on `aspen-place`. Only roots use the concrete
stores. `aspen-compact` (C1) depends only on core and `aspen-pack`: it
reaches metadata and blobs through the core traits, and a root runs its
worker. Enforce this with a `cargo deny` / workspace check in CI.

---

## 3. Execution graph

**Delivery mode:** merged PRs into `main` of this repository. The coordinator
(the Aspen Reason's main Session) owns the plan, reviews every exact PR head,
and merges one PR at a time. Each node gets its own child Session, its own
branch and one focused PR. Run every ready node in parallel.

**Required CI on `main`, kept fast:**

- `cargo fmt`, `clippy -D warnings`, `cargo test`
- `cargo doc` with rustdoc warnings denied (in the `clippy + rustdoc + layering` job)
- the interop smoke (current Git, protocol v2, over HTTP)
- `cargo deny`

Everything slow runs nightly: the full interop matrix, chaos, fuzzing and
benchmarks.

### Wave 0: foundation (coordinator, sequential)

| ID | Node | Done when |
|---|---|---|
| N0 | Bootstrap: workspace, CI, rustfmt/clippy config, `aspen dev` stub, LICENSE placeholder (license decided before going public) | CI green on `main` |
| S1 | Spike: stitched packs accepted by git 2.30 / 2.39 / 2.43 / 2.47 / latest, JGit, libgit2, go-git, gitoxide. Cover duplicate objects across segments and thin bases. | Matrix + script in `bench/spikes/s1/` |
| S2 | Spike: push semantics with trunk-only advertisement (create, update, force, delete, `--force-with-lease`, atomic pushes) | Transcripts + decision recorded here |
| S3 | Spike: `packfile-uri` across client versions, including an interrupted download and resume | Matrix |
| S4 | Spike: streaming `gix-pack` ingest of a multi-GB pack: throughput and memory, including the cost of thin-pack completion (spool, base lookup, second hash-and-upload pass; push step 2) | Numbers |
| S5 | Spike: shallow and filter requests, and what stock clients do when they aren't advertised | Decision |
| N1 | Contracts in `aspen-core` plus schema v1, revised from the spikes | Reviewed, merged |

Spikes S1–S5 run in parallel child Sessions. N1 waits for all of them.

### Wave 1: after N1, all in parallel

| ID | Node | Deps |
|---|---|---|
| A1 | `aspen-proto` | N1 |
| A2 | `aspen-pack`: ingest, index, commit extraction, stitcher | N1, S1 |
| A3 | `aspen-meta`: Postgres + SQLite, compare-and-swap, commit index, jobs | N1 |
| A4 | `aspen-blob` | N1 |
| A5 | `aspen-planner` + property tests against a `git rev-list --objects` oracle | N1 |
| A6 | `testkit`: differential oracle, client matrix images | N0 |
| A7 | `bench/`: `aspen-bench` load generator plus baselines (`git http-backend`, Gitea, Forgejo, GitLab Gitaly via Docker) | N0 |
| A8 | `deploy/compose`, plus Terraform AWS infrastructure (network, RDS, S3, CloudFront, ECS/NLB) with a placeholder app | N0 |
| A9 | `demo/place` front end against a mock API | N0 |

### Wave 2: services

| ID | Node | Deps |
|---|---|---|
| A1b | v0 upload-pack in `aspen-proto` for clients without v2 fetch (libgit2, go-git): v0 advertisement, streaming want/shallow/deepen/have/done parser under A1's byte limits, ACK/NAK for stateless HTTP (decisions log, 2026-11-03) | A1 |
| B1 | receive-pack service; measures push latency with thin-pack completion against `git receive-pack`. A2's base prefetch budget (`LocateOptions::max_prefetch_bytes`) is per call, so B1 shares one prefetch budget across concurrent pushes or caps how many run at once | A1–A4 |
| B2 | upload-pack v2: ls-refs, planning, packfile-uri + stitched fallback; plans every shallow request in `FullClosure` (no haves); exports `PlanStats.visited` as a metric | A1, A3–A5 |
| B3 | Auth, multi-repo, quotas, rate limits | A1, A3 |

### Wave 3: production features, all in parallel

| ID | Node | Deps |
|---|---|---|
| C1 | Compaction worker + skip-list planning, including the have-side skip through verified cover chains (known gap from A5: the walk is linear in versions until then) | B1, A5 |
| C2 | `aspen-express` + CDN mode | B2 |
| C3 | SSH transport | B1, B2 |
| C4 | Mirror mode (GitHub App/token import, proxy + background push-through, conflict reporting) | B1, B2 |
| C5 | Observability: OTel, Prometheus, Grafana JSON; dead-job and wider-trunk-version metrics; `aspen doctor jobs` | B1, B2 |
| C6 | Helm; Terraform for GCP, Azure and Hetzner+MinIO; release pipeline (multi-arch images, Homebrew, `cargo install`) | B1–B3 |
| C7 | Demo back end: `place` repo rules, merger bot, live event feed | B1–B3 |
| C8 | Exact cached shallow packs: build the pack for `(ref_version, depth)` once with the C1 repack writer, store it, and serve it to every later shallow fetch of that tip, keeping the over-fetch path as fallback. Done when the depth-1 fetch-storm benchmark shows bytes served per fetch at most 1.2× stock `git upload-pack`, with the differential suite and `fsck --strict` green | B2, C1, A5 |

### Wave 4: hardening and launch

| ID | Node |
|---|---|
| D1 | Chaos suite |
| D2 | Benchmark campaign + write-up, including the stale-branch fetch (`PlanStats.visited` against N) and real bytes against stock `git upload-pack` |

| D4 | Security review of the public endpoint |
| D5 | Public deployment (section 6) |
| D6 | Docs site, "run it yourself in 5 minutes", launch post draft |
| L0 | Differential suite and client matrix against a real Aspen server, not only stock `git http-backend`: a launch-gate prerequisite for items 1 and 2 (section 7). Depends on merged `main` (B1, B2, C1, C8). Fixes or files every divergence found (label `follow-up`) |

---

## 4. Testing: how reliability is earned

1. **Differential testing.** Generate random operation sequences (push, force,
   delete, branch, merge, concurrent pushes) and run each against Aspen and
   stock `git http-backend`. After every step, ref state and reachable objects
   must match, and `git fsck --strict` must pass on a fresh clone from each.
   Pushes and races go through branch-scoped URLs and, with
   `--plain-url-pushes` (required CI's Aspen slice and the nightly Aspen
   rows), through the plain repository URL too, where Aspen sees a zero old
   value and decides fast-forwards itself (#135).
   `testkit/scripts/check-aspen-mutations.py` must catch each of its faults
   (L1 to L4: a pack missing an object, a skipped stale-old check, a fetch
   plan missing a pack, a plain-URL non-fast-forward accepted without force).
2. **Planner property tests.** Over random ref-version histories and covers,
   the selected packs must hold every object of `git rev-list --objects
   <wants>` that is not in `git rev-list --objects <haves>` (not the
   `<wants> ^<haves>` form, which also lists objects the haves reach through
   older commits; A5). Bound how much over-fetching is allowed: A5 requires
   the plan to hold exactly the objects of the packs of the versions
   reachable from a want and not from a have. Haves must include non-tip commits of multi-commit pushes, tips
   of wider-closure versions (a branch created at an old trunk commit, a
   forced update), and commits the server has never seen: the fetch service
   resolves each through `fetch::HaveVersion` or drops it, and none may lose
   an object (N1 review).
3. **Interop matrix (nightly):**
   - Clients: git 2.30 → latest, Apple Git, JGit, libgit2, go-git and gitoxide.
   - Every combination of protocol v0/v2, HTTP/SSH, and `packfile-uri` on/off.
   - Port the relevant t55xx transport tests from Git's own suite.
4. **Chaos testing** ([`tests/chaos`](../tests/chaos/README.md), node D1):
   - **Faults:** kill servers mid-push and mid-compaction; inject S3 errors
     and latency (the suite's own HTTP fault proxy in front of MinIO); fail
     over Postgres (to a synchronous standby); partition the network
     (between clients and Aspen, and between Aspen and Postgres or S3).
   - **Invariants:** an acknowledged push is never lost; no ref ever points at
     objects that can't be fetched; history per ref is linearizable, checked
     with a compare-and-swap-register linearizability checker.
5. **Fuzzing** (cargo-fuzz): pkt-line, pack ingest (delta cycles, zip bombs,
   huge objects), the stitcher and the planner; the HTTP and SSH front ends
   (the route parser, SSH commands, gzip request bodies, JWT verification;
   #139); SHA-1 collisions are out of a fuzzer's reach (decisions log, D3).
   Targets and how to run them:
   [fuzz/README.md](../fuzz/README.md).
6. **Performance:**
   - Reproduce OpenAI's simulated workload: N writers on distinct branches,
     measuring push→fetch latency.
   - Add a fetch storm (1,000 jobs fetching one commit) and a clone at line
     rate.
   - Add a **depth-1 fetch storm**: 1,000 `git fetch --depth=1` jobs (the
     `actions/checkout` default) for one commit. Report bytes served per fetch
     and total egress next to stock `git upload-pack`. With S5's over-fetch
     each fetch cost a full clone; C8's exact shallow packs bring it to
     stock's bytes (`bench/baselines/run.sh aspen`; decisions log, "C8:
     depth-1 storm"). D2 publishes the numbers from identical instances.
   - Add a **stale-branch fetch**: a client current on trunk fetches a
     branch created N trunk pushes ago, for N up to 10,000. Report
     `PlanStats.visited` (exported by B2) and planning latency next to N,
     with and without compaction; with chained covers the walk is
     logarithmic in N (C1, fetch path).
   - Measure real bytes served against stock `git upload-pack` for the
     same fetches. The planner property tests' planned-to-lacking ratio
     (about 1.9×) is a property of their generator, not a product number.
   - Compare against the baselines on identical instances.
   - Run nightly, with checks for regressions.
7. **Security:**
   - Tenant isolation between repos.
   - Presigned URL scope and expiry.
   - Auth bypass tests.
   - Limits on push size and object count.

---

## 5. Self-hosting experience (part of done, not an afterthought)

- **Try it:** `brew install aspen && aspen dev` runs SQLite plus local disk.
  Then `git clone http://localhost:7070/demo.git`.
- **Single host:** `docker compose up` (Aspen + Postgres + MinIO).
- **AWS:** Terraform for ECS Fargate (or EKS via Helm), RDS Postgres, S3,
  CloudFront and an NLB for SSH.
- **GCP:** Cloud Run, Cloud SQL, GCS and Cloud CDN.
- **Azure:** Container Apps, Flexible Server and Blob storage.
- **Hetzner / bare metal:** MinIO + Postgres.
- **Front GitHub:** `aspen import github.com/org/repo --mode mirror`.
- **Operations:** `aspen doctor` reports health, job lag and pack levels.

Every deploy path gets an end-to-end CI job: provision, clone, push, fetch,
destroy. The cloud jobs run nightly.

---

## 6. Public deployment and demo

### `place`: a public pixel canvas where every brush stroke is a `git push`

- Each visitor gets their own branch, so pushes run in parallel with no
  contention. That is causal consistency, visible.
- A merger bot folds branches into `main` every few seconds. The canvas
  renders `main` plus live branch overlays.
- It is a real repository:
  - `git clone https://place.<domain>/canvas.git`
  - edit `tiles/<x>_<y>.txt`
  - `git push`, and the pixel appears.
- Pre-receive rules accept only tile files in the fixed format, so there is no
  arbitrary content. Quotas apply per IP and per branch, and branches expire.

### The rest of the public site

- **Live dashboard:** pushes/s, p50/p99 push→fetch latency, packs per level,
  bytes served, cache hit rate.
- **"Unleash 1,000 agents" button:** a bounded, rate-limited load burst you can
  watch on the dashboard. It has a kill switch.
- **Benchmarks page:** reproducible numbers and scripts against the baselines.

### Hosting

- Deploy on AWS with this repository's own Terraform module. That proves the
  self-host path.
- Use an AWS account or VPC fully isolated from Reason production. Never use
  production credentials, networks or databases.
- Start on a subdomain we already own. Buying a new domain, or any spend
  beyond the budget below, needs the maintainers' approval.
- **Budget:** set an AWS budget alarm at $300/month, with CloudFront egress
  watched. Rough estimate: $150–300/month idle.
- Bring up staging first, then the public demo.

---

## 7. Launch gate (all true before the demo is public)

- [ ] Differential suite: 0 divergences over 1M random operations.
- [ ] Interop matrix all green.
- [ ] Chaos run of 24 h with no lost acknowledged push.
- [ ] Fuzzers ran 24 h clean.
- [ ] Benchmarks reproducible from scratch by a teammate following the docs.
- [ ] Depth-1 fetch storm measured and published: bytes served per fetch at
      most 1.2× stock `git upload-pack` (needs C8), or the gap stated on the
      benchmarks page with the egress cost at demo traffic.
- [ ] `docker compose up` and AWS Terraform verified from a clean account by
      following the docs only.
- [ ] Public endpoint security review done; quotas and kill switch tested.
- [ ] Budget alarm live.

The license is decided: Apache-2.0 (decisions log, 2026-11-04). Making the
repository public and publishing releases or crates are the maintainers' call. Prepare
them, don't do them.

On 2026-11-04 the maintainers waived this gate for a **pre-alpha** launch: Aspen goes
public with every unchecked item above stated in the README's status section.
Launch checklist for that:

- [ ] Publish as a fresh single-commit copy of `main`'s tree in a **new
      public repository** at github.com/reason-machines/aspen, not by
      switching this repository to public. This repository stays private
      as the full record, renamed reason-machines/aspen (decisions log,
      2026-11-04, "publish from a new repository").
- [ ] #118 (bound
      concurrent fetches, pushes and stitch work) is fixed before the demo
      is public.
- [x] README states the pre-alpha status and every unmet gate item.
- [x] Secret scan: no real secret in the tree or in the history of any
      branch or pull request ref (decisions log, 2026-11-04).

## 8. Decisions log

| Date | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| 2026-11-02 | Name Aspen (working) | Pando is an aspen grove; rename before launch if needed |
| 2026-11-02 | Rust; `gix-*`, axum, sqlx, `object_store`, russh | Correctness + performance; `object_store` gives multi-cloud blobs in one impl |
| 2026-11-02 | Stitched-pack fallback for stock clients | `fetch.uriprotocols` is empty by default (git docs, `technical/packfile-uri`) |
| 2026-11-02 | Merged-PR delivery, child Session per node | Max parallelism with reviewable units |
| 2026-11-03 | N0: toolchain pinned in `rust-toolchain.toml` (1.99.1), edition 2024, all crates `publish = false` until a license is chosen | Reproducible CI; nothing can be published by accident |
| 2026-11-03 | N0: layering rule enforced by `cargo xtask check-deps` (reads `cargo metadata`), not by `cargo deny` | Clear error messages that name the rule; unit-tested. Only `aspen-server`/`aspen-cli` may depend on the concrete backends (`aspen-meta`, `aspen-blob`); other crates use the `aspen-core` traits. Dev-dependencies are exempt |
| 2026-11-03 | N0: interop smoke runs against stock `git http-backend` (std-only CGI bridge in `tests/interop`) until B1/B2 serve Git, then against Aspen too | Keeps the required smoke real from day one and gives A6 a reference server |
| 2026-11-03 | N0: CI exposes one aggregate check, `ci-ok`, for branch protection | Adding or renaming jobs does not require changing repository settings |
| 2026-11-03 | Serve shallow in v1 by over-fetching: full closure of the wants plus exact `shallow-info`. Policy `shallow = serve \| refuse`, default `serve` | S5: without shallow, `actions/checkout` (depth 1) and GitLab Runner (depth 20) defaults fail on git 2.30/2.39/2.56. Over-fetch is accepted by all of them and keeps one stored pack per ref version. Cost is bandwidth; exact cached shallow packs are node C8, tracked by the depth-1 fetch-storm benchmark |
| 2026-11-03 | Shallow requests never use haves in planning | S5: a have-based plan for a shallow client fails on a restored old blob and on a branch forked below the boundary (`did not send all necessary objects`) |
| 2026-11-03 | `commit_index` stores parent oids and committer time | S5: `shallow-info` for depth N, since and exclude needs a parent walk; fast-forward checks need ancestry too |
| 2026-11-03 | Never advertise `filter`; `ERR` on a `filter` line or a non-commit want | S5: all tested clients fall back to a full fetch with a warning. Lazy fetches from existing partial clones send blob-ID wants that `commit_index` can't resolve |
| 2026-11-03 | A `commit_index` walk that reaches an unindexed commit reads it from the ref version's packs; if still unresolved, retryable `ERR`/`ng` | Non-tip rows are written asynchronously; treating a missing row as "no parents" would emit a wrong `shallow-info` or a wrong fast-forward verdict (coordinator review of S5) |
| 2026-11-03 | A8: Aspen reads `DATABASE_URL` without the password plus `PGPASSWORD`, in compose and on AWS alike; on AWS the password is generated ephemerally and set through write-only attributes | The secret can come from Secrets Manager on its own and never enters Terraform state; sqlx and libpq both honour `PGPASSWORD` |
| 2026-11-03 | A8: CloudFront fronts only the pack bucket and requires signed URLs; Git smart HTTP goes straight to the ALB | Packs are private data; smart HTTP is authenticated, uncacheable, and long clones can outlast CloudFront's origin timeout |
| 2026-11-03 | A8: compose uses Chainguard's MinIO image by default (overridable) | Upstream MinIO stopped publishing container images |
| 2026-11-03 | A8: AWS defaults to no NAT gateway (tasks in public subnets with a public IP, ingress only from the load balancers) | Saves about $33/month on a ~$78/month idle footprint; `nat_gateway = true` switches to private subnets |
| 2026-11-03 | A8: Terraform fmt/validate/test (mocked providers)/tflint and `compose config` run in required CI (`deploy-config`, part of `ci-ok`); the compose smoke and the image build run in `deploy.yml` on PRs touching `deploy/` and nightly | Static checks take about a minute and need no credentials; the smoke pulls images and the image build compiles a release, so they stay out of the required path |
| 2026-11-03 | Stored packs are self-contained: thin pushes are completed at ingest, replacing "stored unchanged" | S3: a thin URI pack fails on every client from 2.30 to 2.56 and on OpenAI's fork; advertising the post-`--fix-thin` hash depends on client zlib settings |
| 2026-11-03 | packfile-URI response rules (section 2, fetch path step 3) | S3 matrix: clients don't filter schemes, don't follow redirects, send no credentials, need a `packfile` section, and ≤ 2.55 cannot recover from `416` on resume |
| 2026-11-03 | Thin-pack completion is synchronous: spool to local disk while validating, append missing bases, then hash and multipart-upload the final pack in a second pass; the ref moves only after the self-contained pack and `.idx` are durable | Completion rewrites the header count and trailer, so it cannot be a single streaming pass. Synchronous keeps one invariant (every `packs` row is self-contained) for the URI planner, stitcher and compactor; async with a `self_contained` flag would let a ref point at a pack no planner may name. Cost (disk, push latency) measured by S4/B1 (coordinator review of S3) |
| 2026-11-03 | S1: the stitcher drops repeated object ids (keeps the first copy) and rewrites only OFS_DELTA headers; a stitched pack never repeats an object, never has a delta before its base and is never thin. Replaces "concatenate entries, rewrite the count, recompute the trailer" | S1 matrix (48 client configurations, 21 scenarios): plain concatenation is rejected by libgit2 1.9.7, by git 2.30–2.47 when a repeated object is a REF_DELTA base, and by every git with `fetch.fsckObjects`; go-git v5.19 reports success but leaves a pack stock git can't read. Completed push packs repeat the bases of older packs, so a plain clone of three pushes fails. go-git never asks for `thin-pack` and rejects forward REF_DELTAs. The deduplicating stitcher passes all 48 configurations and 500 random plans |
| 2026-11-03 | S1: stored packs are base-first; thin-pack completion writes the appended bases before the received entries, not after them as `index-pack --fix-thin` does | Keep-first deduplication of base-first, self-contained segments never has to hold an entry back, so stitching stays one streaming pass (checked on all 155 sequences of 1–3 such segments). With bases appended last, dropping a repeated base forces a reorder (14 of 30 entries in the spike's completed push) |
| 2026-11-03 | S1: ingest repairs, never rejects, a received pack with a forward base (a REF_DELTA before its base): pass 2 moves each such entry, and entries based on it, to just after its base and recomputes the OFS_DELTA distances that changed; a forward base found later in a stored pack fails the fetch with a retryable `ERR` that is also a paging alert, fixed by rewriting the pack base-first (coordinator review of S1) | Stock `receive-pack` accepts forward bases, so rejecting them breaks pushes that work elsewhere. Pass 1 already resolves every delta and the spool file is local, so the repair is cheap. No client sent one in S1's push check (git 2.30–2.56, JGit, libgit2, go-git; 32 pushes, `bench/spikes/s1/push_order.py`), so this path guards unknown clients. A retry can't fix a bad stored pack, so it must page |
| 2026-11-03 | S1: naive deduplication (keep first copy, OFS_DELTA to REF_DELTA) is rejected | It made two-object delta cycles: git 2.56 and JGit accepted the pack and the client's fsck then exhausted memory (27 GB observed); libgit2 exhausted memory too |
| 2026-11-03 | Object location (thin-pack bases, S2's existence check) probes the `.idx` of the trunk version's covering packs, newest level first; no global `oid → pack` table in v1 | `commit_index` covers commits only. Probe cost is missing bases × packs × log N, with packs bounded by compaction levels; a per-object table is billions of rows and a write per object (S3 README, "Base-object lookup") |
| 2026-11-03 | Direct-to-bucket packfile URIs only for clients whose `agent=` is `git/2.56` or later; others get `aspen-express`/CDN URIs with the empty-`206` rule, or the stitched pack. Refined by C2: a CDN tier serves older clients only when declared `ASPEN_CDN_PAST_END=206` (row "C2: delivery order per planned pack") | S3 E5: git ≤ 2.55 is stuck for good after an interrupted `index-pack` when the bucket answers `416`; Debian 12 ships 2.39. The OpenAI fork reports `git/2.55.0.921.g…` and so is treated as 2.55. Agent is self-reported: a reliability choice, not security |
| 2026-11-03 | S2: a non-fast-forward to an unadvertised ref needs `-o force`, `-o force=<ref>` or a matching `-o lease=<ref>:<oid>`; the repository's `allow_force` policy (default `branches`, never trunk) only gates whether force is possible. Replaces "or the repository allows force-pushes" | `git push --force` sends exactly the same bytes as a plain push when the branch was not advertised, so a permissive policy alone would let any stale agent overwrite a branch silently |
| 2026-11-03 | S2: advertise up to 8 earlier trunk tips as `.have` lines | A client behind trunk otherwise pushes its whole history (16 objects vs 4 in the spike). All `.have`s are trunk ancestors, so the dependency stays one trunk version |
| 2026-11-03 | S2: a push depends on the trunk version current when it commits; trunk is fast-forward-only by default; B1 checks only objects the pack references but lacks against the dependency closure, using S3's base-object lookup (batched); no reachability walk | Smart HTTP is stateless, so the server cannot know which trunk tip the client saw. A trunk rewind between advertisement and push left a pack missing 2 objects relative to the new trunk |
| 2026-11-03 | S2: lease and delete for stock clients through `-o lease=` and a branch-scoped URL `/<repo>.git/-/refs/heads/<branch>`; full-name delete gated like a force (row below). `explicit-haves` (OpenAI's Git fork) deferred past v1 | Stock clients refuse `--force-with-lease` ("stale info") and `--delete <short>` for unadvertised refs before contacting the server. `explicit-haves` fixes both but needs a forked client and an O(refs) advertisement |
| 2026-11-03 | S2: thin push packs accepted on the wire, completed at ingest (S3's rule); `no-thin` stays an optional per-repository setting | Stock clients send thin packs; `no-thin` makes them send every edited file whole (806 B vs 413 B in the spike, more on large files). Completing at ingest keeps every stored pack self-contained, which packfile URIs need (S3), so fetch never hands out a pack whose bases the client lacks. Settled by S3, not left open |
| 2026-11-03 | S2: an unscoped delete (zero old-oid) of an existing ref needs `-o force` and is gated by `allow_force`; a delete with a real old-oid (scoped URL or lease) is a compare-and-swap delete | A zero old-oid delete is unconditional, the same staleness problem as an invisible `--force`. The scoped URL and the lease give a real old-oid, so a safe delete stays available without force (coordinator review of S2) |
| 2026-11-03 | S2: commit-check verdicts are computed against the exact `ref_version` the compare-and-swap expects; on a lost CAS, recompute (bounded) or retryable `ng` | A verdict computed against a stale version could commit a non-fast-forward as a fast-forward (coordinator review of S2) |
| 2026-11-03 | S2: the object lookup for thin-base completion and the connectivity check searches the packs of every ref version advertised on the connection (trunk, plus the branch for a branch-scoped URL), which are exactly the push's `deps`. Extends S3's object-location row above, which named only the trunk version | A client builds its pack against what it was shown. With a scoped URL it was shown the branch too, so missing bases and referenced objects may live only in that branch's packs; searching trunk alone would reject valid pushes (coordinator review of S2) |
| 2026-11-03 | A9: `place` API is REST + server-sent events, specified in `demo/place/api/openapi.yaml`; one global `seq` orders pushes and merges, snapshots carry it, and `GET /events?after=<seq>` resumes (or asks the client to `resync`) | SSE works through CDNs and proxies and needs no client library; a single sequence lets the page line up snapshot, stream and its own push responses without races. C7 implements the spec, and a contract test keeps the mock honest |
| 2026-11-03 | A9: tile file format is exactly `#rrggbb\n` (lowercase, palette color, mode 100644) at `tiles/<x>_<y>.txt` with canonical decimal coordinates; no deletes, no merge commits, at most 64 tiles per push (`demo/place/docs/TILES.md`) | One byte-exact form means the pre-receive check is a fixed-size compare plus a palette lookup, and every tile has one canonical path, so merges never need content merging |
| 2026-11-03 | A9: front end is Vite + TypeScript with no runtime dependencies and no web fonts; dev tooling (Vite, TypeScript, Vitest, Biome, yaml) pinned exactly with a lockfile; its CI job runs in parallel with the Rust jobs | Small, fast page on phones; reproducible offline builds; about a minute of CI that does not lengthen the required run |
| 2026-11-03 | A9 review: obligations for C7's `place` back end. (1) Operators authenticate to the kill switch with real operator credentials, never a static token in production (the mock's fixed token is for tests only). (2) The swarm is one global run enforced server-side: `maxAgents`, `pushesPerSecondCap`, cooldown and starts per address apply whatever the client sends. (3) Engaging the kill switch ends the swarm and also pauses ambient agents, as the mock does. (4) The pre-receive range is `<new tip> ^<trunk tip> ^<stored branch tip>` read server-side, never the client's old-oid, and the merger bot replays tile sets onto trunk as one fast-forward commit, never a `git merge` (`demo/place/docs/TILES.md`) | A runaway public demo must not be able to spend the $300/month AWS budget or be switched back on by a visitor; the client's old-oid is zero for unadvertised branches (S2), so trusting it would recount a branch's earlier pushes toward the 64-tile cap |
| 2026-11-03 | S2 follow-up: the trunk version recorded in a push's `deps` is the exact version step 2's lookup and connectivity check searched, not trunk re-read at commit time. Replaces "the trunk version current when it commits" in the S2 row above | Where trunk force is allowed, trunk can be rewound between the connectivity check and the commit; recording the re-read version would commit a ref version whose referenced objects were checked against a different closure, breaking the `ref_versions` connectivity invariant (coordinator review of S2) |
| 2026-11-03 | A7: the GitLab baseline is the Omnibus image (nginx → Workhorse → Gitaly, Rails authorization), not a standalone Gitaly | Gitaly only speaks gRPC; Git over HTTP needs Workhorse, which pre-authorizes every request against Rails. A hand-built HTTP front would not be what GitLab users run. See `bench/README.md` |
| 2026-11-03 | A7: push→fetch latency runs from the writer's successful `git push` exit to the end of an independent reader's `git fetch` that returns the tip; every acknowledged push gets its own reader (elastic pool) and reader queueing is reported separately | Matches OpenAI's "when an independent reader can check out each commit"; a fixed reader pool made the metric measure the load generator |
| 2026-11-03 | A7: stored benchmark baselines are kept per machine class (`bench/baselines/results/<class>/<target>/`) and compared with loose tolerances (`bench/tolerances.json`) | Numbers from different hardware are not comparable; shared CI runners are noisy, so the nightly check catches large regressions only |

Both are ordinary. A wrong `Exact` breaks fetches; a wrong `Wider` costs bytes, and not only once: a version that depends on a wider one is wider, so one wider trunk version makes every later trunk version, and every branch built on trunk, wider for good, which turns every incremental fetch of the repository into a full clone. Trunk must therefore never become wider in ordinary use (see the per-write pack row below). A forced trunk update (only possible when `allow_force = all`) is made exact at push time by B1 (row "Forced trunk update" below); until B1 lands it, it makes trunk wider for good. A refusal there keeps the current-tip refusal; undecided ancestry stays the retryable `cannot decide ancestry yet`. receive-pack reads that one earlier version only when `refs::replay_parent` names it, so an ordinary push reads nothing more. The match is on outcome (same old, same new, made by the immediately previous version), not on request identity, so it must not accept a command its own rules would refuse: the first version of this fix answered `ok` before the force, fast-forward and tag checks, so a non-fast-forward without force rights that someone else had already forced got `ok` (coordinator review of #169). | 2026-11-04 | Issue #134: receive-pack reads push bodies sent with `Content-Encoding: gzip` (or `x-gzip`), inflating them as the pack streams in (`aspen_server::body::GzipPush`); any other encoding is still `415`. Settles B3's obligation for compressed push bodies: such a push is charged to the per-push cap and the pushed-bytes-per-hour budget as the **larger of its sent and inflated bytes so far**, so the guard's `PushMeter` is handed to receive-pack (`EncodedPushMeter`) instead of wrapping the compressed body. The `413` check on a declared `Content-Length` still compares the length as sent, the only number known before the body is read. With no quota configured, a gzip body may send at most what an uncompressed one could carry: the pack limit plus 1/1024 of it plus twice the request limit (`ReceivePack::max_sent_bytes`); over that the push fails with `unpack error` naming the limit. Charging inflated bytes keeps the quota a bound on what the server parses, completes and stores: a body under the cap as sent could otherwise inflate to many times it. Charging sent bytes too keeps a stream that inflates to nothing (empty deflate blocks) from being read for free, and the sent-bytes ceiling does the same when no quota is set. Charging only inflated bytes, or only wire bytes as the issue's second option proposed, each leaves one of those open. Tests in `crates/aspen-server/tests/compressed_push.rs`: a gzip push (and a thin `x-gzip` one) is stored as if sent plain; a cut-short gzip body and an unknown encoding store nothing; a body under the cap as sent that inflates past it, and a second push that fits the budget compressed but not inflated, are refused and store nothing; a never-inflating stream is cut off by the sent-bytes ceiling and by the cap. All fail on `main` (`415`), and the cap, budget and never-inflating cases each fail when the meter counts only sent or only inflated bytes, or the ceiling is removed. Falling back to the peer is limited like any address. A hop count set too high is not caught by the fallback: Aspen then reads an entry the client wrote, so a client that sends its own header still chooses its key; only callers that send no header fall back to the proxy's bucket (`missing`). The default list covers the load balancers Aspen ships for (AWS ALB in the VPC, in-cluster ingress, platform front ends); a proxy on public addresses needs an explicit list. It also trusts any other host on a private address that reaches Aspen around the proxy (another pod, a tailnet client), an accepted risk in `docs/SECURITY.md`: the chart and deploy README say to narrow it to the proxy's addresses, and the AWS module lets only the ALB reach the HTTP port. Forwarded IPv4-mapped entries (`::ffff:a.b.c.d`) are read as their IPv4 address.
