(3) Bound: a superseded version's exact shallow pack is deleted at most `shallow_ttl + gc_grace + 2 × gc_every` (2 h 20 min, `CompactConfig::reclaim_bound`) after it was superseded, an orphan `gc_grace + gc_every` after its last registration: one interval for the pass that evicts (the eviction restarts the grace period), one for the pass that sweeps once grace is over; it holds while each pass finishes what is due, at most `GC_ROUNDS × gc_batch` items per step within the budget, and a larger backlog drains over later passes. (4) The packfile URL cap is enforced in code and tied to grace: `aspen_core::pack::MAX_PACK_URL_LIFETIME` (1 hour) is the one constant behind `RECOMMENDED_MAX_PRESIGN_TTL` (the fetch path clamps every presign to it) and express's `MAX_URL_LIFETIME` (it refuses a longer policy), and `CompactConfig::validate`, run by `aspen serve` at startup, refuses `gc_grace` under twice it and `gc_batch = 0`. (5) Trade-offs of a 2-hour grace: every writer must commit within 2 hours of registering its pack (a push, a compaction job recording a cover, an exact shallow build); a slower one is refused retryably (`StoreError::pack_swept`: an `Unavailable` whose reason starts with a fixed prefix, recognised by `is_pack_swept`, which reads the same constant) and registers and uploads its pack again on retry, counted as `aspen_push_grace_exceeded_total` and `aspen_shallow_builds_total{result="grace_exceeded"}` with a `warn` log (a compaction job records the error on its job row and is retried). A stitched fetch still opening an evicted shallow pack 2 hours after the eviction fails and the client retries. (6) `Wider` inheritance: a write depending on a `Wider` version is `Wider`, so after a lost compare-and-swap across a forced update makes a branch version `Wider` (`closure_exact = 0`), later plain-URL fast-forwards of that branch stay `Wider` until a forced update or a scoped-URL push that does not depend on it. This adds little: a plain-URL push of a branch whose tip does not contain trunk's tip is already `Wider` through its trunk dependency (in `storage.rs` 34 of 35 versions of both the plain-URL and the scoped-URL branch). Depending only on exact versions was tried and undoes (1) (9.23x). Counted as `aspen_push_branch_versions_total{closure}`. Dropping held objects never leaves a delta without its base: own and shared packs keep no held base (`receive::push::keep_flags`), and `aspen_pack::subset::write_subset` stores a delta whose base is not kept whole (`a_delta_whose_base_is_left_out_is_stored_whole`). With (1), push packs are 48.7 MB and the bucket 821 MB, of which 719 MB are exact shallow packs of versions superseded within seconds, kept for 48 hours by the old defaults. 2 hours of grace is twice the longest life of any packfile URL, so every URL handed out before an eviction has expired an hour before the deletion. | 2026-11-06 | **Merge queue** (`aspen_server::queue`, `ASPEN_MERGE_QUEUE`, off by default). Clients push a change to their own ref under `refs/queue/<trunk branch>/` through the plain URL (an ordinary branch-like write; the namespace is writable only while the queue is on, and only the queue writes the outcome namespaces). A worker in every instance takes pending entries oldest first, up to `ASPEN_MERGE_QUEUE_BATCH`; for each it finds the base (first commit on the entry's first-parent line that is an ancestor of trunk, by the push path's exact ancestry walk), diffs base→entry and base→trunk per file (a directory that appears or goes away is its files appearing or going away, so changes adding different files to one new directory both land), rejects the entry if a path equals or is nested in one trunk or an earlier batch entry changed, and otherwise writes one trunk commit (entry's tree changes applied to the batch's tree, author and message kept, committer the queue; several commits squash into one). The batch lands as one `--atomic` in-process receive-pack push: trunk T→new tip, each queue ref deleted against the commit the worker read, each outcome ref created (`refs/queue-done/...` at the landed commit, `refs/queue-rejected/...` at a report commit on trunk naming the original commit and the conflicting paths) | Unbatched landings are bounded by one fetch-rebase-push cycle per trunk update, however many clients push (bench/throughput); folding every pending change into one compare-and-swap removes that bound without changing the push model: trunk still moves only by fast-forward through receive-pack, and the queue's writes are checked, completed and stored like any push. Exactly once: the atomic push lands a batch together with its queue-ref deletes or not at all, and each delete is a compare-and-swap on the commit the batch used, so a lost race (a direct trunk push, another instance's worker, a client re-pushing its entry) refuses the whole batch and it is rebuilt from what is stored; no single-runner assumption. Two workers that build the same batch in the same second write identical commits, and the second push is a no-op replay (issue #132). The rejection report is a commit on trunk rather than a tag of the rejected commit, because the in-process push depends on trunk only and may not name objects only the deleted queue ref's closure holds. (2) **Boundary outside the dependencies.** The client never says which ref it will push, so an acknowledged commit can be on a ref the push does not depend on: a branch created from another branch, an amended forced push (forced updates do not depend on the branch), a merge into trunk, a tag, a new branch at an existing commit, several refs at once. The writes' dependencies do not change: each write's pack is then planned by the forced-trunk walk, generalised (`Push::fill`), and holds everything its tip reaches that its dependencies lack, copied from storage where the client left it out. | 2026-11-07 | Throughput item 6b: **a branch push sends PostgreSQL at most 5 statements** (4 in the steady state: the advertisement 1, the push 3), from 19 (21 with the two repository lookups, which item 6a caches). (1) **Reads.** The push reads trunk, the ref it builds on and every command's ref in one statement (`MetaStore::current_versions`), and **the commit check uses those versions** instead of reading each ref again after ingest; a verdict on a version that moved since loses its compare-and-swap and is recomputed against the new one (push path, step 4), so this changes cost, not outcomes; it makes losing a race on a hot trunk a little more likely. The search set's closure walk reads a chain of versions it has not seen in one recursive statement that stops at the roots it remembers (`MetaStore::reachable_versions`, at most 512 rows; behind three servers it was one read per version, about 15 per push), and a committed version's closure is remembered when its dependencies' are, so the next push to that branch on the same instance reads none. The advertisement reads trunk and its recent versions (item 10's dense window) in one statement (`MetaStore::current_and_recent`), only those above the newest it remembers, and takes item 10's other `.have` versions from the closure cache (`Closures::versions_at`), reading only those this instance lacks. A pack a push stored is put into the `.idx` cache (item 11's `StoredPacks::remember_pack`, which replaced this item's `remember_idx`), so the next push to that ref neither reads its row nor its file. (2) **Writes on PostgreSQL.** A push of one ref commits in **one statement with no transaction**: it takes an ID from the version table's identity sequence, moves the `refs` row to it with compare-and-swap (only if the pack, locked `FOR KEY SHARE`, still exists), inserts the version from the moved row, and writes its commit rows (up to 100) and jobs, all or nothing; foreign keys are checked at the end of the statement, so `refs_version_fk` sees the version it inserted. A lost race now writes nothing without an error (the `UPDATE` re-reads the row after the winner commits). An atomic multi-ref push keeps the transaction (its commit rows need one global order). A pack registration is one statement too: it inserts at generation 0 only when the statement's snapshot holds neither a row nor a tombstone for the checksum, and otherwise locks and touches the existing row; anything else (a tombstone, a row swept or inserted concurrently) falls back to the transaction, which still reads the tombstones after its insert. This relies on a sweep deleting only rows whose last registration is a grace period old, so they are in the snapshot: true while servers' clocks agree within `gc_grace`, which pushes already need. SQLite keeps every write in its transaction (no data-modifying `WITH`). (3) **Group commit** (replaces "every push (or pack) runs behind its own savepoint" in the group commit row): on PostgreSQL a push or pack that is one statement runs in the batch's transaction without a savepoint, so a batch of N pushes is N statements plus `BEGIN` and `COMMIT`; if any statement fails, the transaction is rolled back and the batch runs again with a savepoint around every push, so a failure still refuses only its own push. (4) **Counting**: every store statement runs on `aspen_meta::conn::Conn`, which counts it (`SqlStore::statements`, `MetaStore::count_statements`), reported per push in `Timings::db_queries` and `aspen_push_db_statements_total{kind}`. No index was missing: auto_explain on the push statements shows index scans only, and the recursive read uses `ref_versions_pkey` | Item 6 of the push-throughput work: with three servers, branch pushes plateaued at about 445/s with PostgreSQL (db.m7g.xlarge) at 99.5% CPU and the servers at 25%, about 30 statements and 7 ms of database CPU per push. | 2026-11-06 | **Layered shallow answers** (monorepo item 9). A shallow fetch eligible for an exact pack whose version has none yet waits for the build up to `ShallowCacheConfig::layered_wait` (500 ms), then is served the version's **layered answer** if it has one (`aspen_server::upload_pack::layered`): one stitched pack, never packfile URIs, of a **patch pack** and a **base**. The base is the exact pack recorded for the newest earlier version of the same ref at the same depth (found from a per-process hint, else by probing up to 64 versions back; at most 256 back), with its own commits and tags left out by the stitcher (`StitchPlan::leaving_out`, found from its entry table). The patch holds every other object the region reaches (the new commits and tags, the trees and blobs changed since, and anything only older history holds), read from the packs pushed since or the version's closure (receive-pack's closure cache), written whole, registered and uploaded as a level-1 compaction pack no cover or recording names. Every object sent resolves its links: the base's trees and blobs are closed under reachability, and the only commits are the new region's. A first version sent the base whole plus the pushes' own packs; the chaos smoke's clients (`fetch.fsckObjects`) refused it ("did not receive expected object": the earlier commits' parents and the intermediate trees' entries were not sent). The patch is found by a walk that stops at any tree or blob of the base, so it reads only the commits and trees that changed since the base, one batch per tree level, under `max_layered_bytes` (256 MiB, also the patch's cap). Only a request with one want is served a layered answer (a left-out commit could be another want's). The answer is computed once per version and depth per process (reused 60 s) and is also the exact build's sources, which replaces #266's incremental sources (whose build fell back to the full closure on any missing object, such as a revert). A request whose build slot is busy or whose last build failed is served the layered answer too. Without one (a cold start: no recorded version within reach) the request waits up to `build_wait` (20 s) and falls back to the full closure as before. New metrics: `aspen_shallow_exact_fetches_total{pack="layered"}`, `aspen_shallow_layered_patch_objects_total`, `aspen_shallow_wait_seconds` | The monorepo benchmark (300,000 files, 11.6 M objects): a new trunk tip's exact-pack build took longer than the 20 s wait, so depth-1 clones fell back to the full history (1.7 GB in 109 s, about 3 GB server memory each; 16 CI clones OOM-killed the server), and trunk moves on every landing. A build copies and verifies a whole snapshot, so it cannot fit a sub-second budget at that size; serving the previous snapshot plus the landings since can, and is at most one snapshot plus what changed. Measured on this 4-vCPU machine, local blob store, with `crates/aspen-server/tests/shallow_scale.rs` (300,000 files eight levels deep, 250 MB depth-1 pack, 750 MB of history, 16 concurrent `git fetch --depth=1` after one landing and after five landings plus a revert): on `main` one run sent 16 of 16 clones after the revert the full 750 MB history (16 `wait_timed_out` fallbacks, server heap +1.4 GB) and another kept every clone waiting for its build (slowest clone 44 s against 32.5 s once recorded); with layered answers the longest server wait was 0.70 s and 1.30 s, 0 fallbacks, every clone 250 MB, heap at most +736 MB for all 16 clones. Required CI: `a_new_tip_is_served_the_last_recorded_pack_and_the_pushes_since` and `a_revert_is_patched_into_the_layered_answer` (each fails with layered answers turned off: the fetch falls back to the full history; the second also fails without the patch: `fatal: missing blob object`; both fail when the base's commits are not left out: `fatal: did not receive expected object`; their clients fetch with `fetch.fsckObjects`), and `left_out_objects_are_dropped_from_every_segment` in aspen-pack.
